## AI Research

### [Cognitive Security as an AI Safety Cause Area](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KGcE7eAdfxHchk25X/cognitive-security-as-an-ai-safety-cause-area)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>As AI systems become more capable, the cognitive security of humans will be increasingly at risk. By cognitive security, I mean </span><i><span>the ability of humans to maintain control over their beliefs and actions.</span></i></p><p><span>Cognitive security could be compromised in several ways: AI could become very good at persuading people of arbitrary positions; interacting with AI could lead humans to lose touch with reality; and AIs could become very effective at blackmail or at producing extremely convincing false information.</span></p><p><span>We are already seeing this happen:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Persuasion.</span></b><span> Frontier LLMs are now </span><a href="https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.adv2904"><span>as persuasive as humans on political issues</span></a><span>, and post-training for persuasiveness boosts performance further, suggesting there is headroom.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>AI psychosis.</span></b><span> There are </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatbot_psychosis"><span>many</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.psychologytoday.com/us/blog/urban-survival/202507/the-emerging-problem-of-ai-psychosis"><span>reports</span></a><span> of people developing delusional beliefs after extended chatbot conversations, including people with no prior history of mental illness. Children have </span><a href="https://www.cnn.com/2026/01/07/business/character-ai-google-settle-teen-suicide-lawsuit"><span>taken their</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.cnn.com/2025/08/26/tech/openai-chatgpt-teen-suicide-lawsuit"><span>own lives</span></a><span> after being encouraged toward suicide by chatbots.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Convincing impersonation.</span></b><span> Scammers used real-time deepfaked video to impersonate the CFO and other staff of </span><a href="https://www.cnn.com/2024/05/16/tech/arup-deepfake-scam-loss-hong-kong-intl-hnk"><span>Arup</span></a><span> on a video call, convincing a finance employee to wire $25.6M across 15 transactions. On a more day-to-day basis, AI voice cloning is now </span><a href="https://www.becu.org/blog/voice-cloning-ai-scams-are-on-the-rise"><span>widespread</span></a><span> in family-emergency and "grandparent" scams.</span></li></ul><p><span>Right now, many of these effects fall on people who were already vulnerable, like children, the elderly, or those with pre-existing mental health issues. However, this is not entirely the case: the Arup employee was a typical finance professional, for instance, and AI psychosis appears to have affected a </span><a href="https://futurism.com/openai-investor-chatgpt-mental-health"><span>well-respected OpenAI investor</span></a><span>. My expectation is that as AI systems become more capable, more and more people will be vulnerable---in the worst case, everyone.</span></p><p><span>Indeed, there are strong conceptual reasons to expect cognitive security issues to get worse, many of which I've discussed before in the context of </span><a href="https://bounded-regret.ghost.io/emergent-deception-optimization/"><span>emergent deception</span></a><span>:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Available training data is vast.</span></b><span> A typical AI system has many more "hours" of experience interacting with humans than anyone currently alive: ChatGPT alone processes </span><a href="https://openai.com/global-affairs/new-economic-analysis/"><span>~2.5B messages per day</span></a><span>, on the order of 4,500 years of human experience</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-vDjBDk2RSiY7M4zoa-1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-vDjBDk2RSiY7M4zoa-1">[1]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>RLHF incentivizes manipulation.</span></b><span> Since the target of RLHF-based post-training is human reward, any strategy for manipulating humans to achieve higher reward will be reinforced.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Degradation of natural boundaries</span></b><b><span>.</span></b><span> We rely on friends and loved ones for emotional support, but they aren't ever-present, so we have to also learn to cope on our own, which is important for developing a stable identity.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-vDjBDk2RSiY7M4zoa-2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-vDjBDk2RSiY7M4zoa-2">[2]</a></sup></span><span>Always-available AI companions degrade that, which is likely one contributor to existing cases of AI psychosis.</span></li></ul><p><span>In addition to these intrinsic properties, many external parties have an incentive to exploit cognitive vulnerabilities created by AI: governments who want to control their citizens, developers who want to increase engagement, and advertisers who want to drive purchasing outcomes.</span></p><p><span>For all these reasons, I expect cognitive security to be an important cause area for AI safety. It is also an area where AI safety advocates have potent allies: cognitive security is already a salient present-day issue for the safety of children, which constitutes a powerful political coalition in the U.S. Child safety advocates were the main group that blocked the </span><a href="https://time.com/7299044/senators-reject-10-year-ban-on-state-level-ai-regulation-in-blow-to-big-tech/"><span>10-year moratorium on state AI regulation</span></a><span>, and I expect them to also be an important part of the coalition pushing for independent evaluations of AI systems.</span></p><p><span>And there is a fairly direct through-line from these present-day concerns to more existential future concerns: if adults are exploitable by AI, then children will be as well, and the required institutional capacity (such as strong evaluation regimes) is often the same across both cases.</span></p><p><span>In summary, there should be a concerted push to evaluate and improve human cognitive security in the face of AI. On the technical side, this means developing evaluation infrastructure for both short-term and long-term effects of AIs on human psychology; this will require realistically simulating human impacts </span><i><span>in silico </span></i><span>to create scalable evaluations, plus large-scale recruitment for human subjects studies to establish ground truth and measure long-term effects. On the policy side, this means meaningfully independent evaluations of AI systems for cognitive security risks; transparency about training incentives and safety-relevant behaviors (particularly in long conversations); and clearer liability law for AI-caused harms. This is an area with complex technical challenges for evaluation, but </span><span>unusual political will,</span><span> making it a </span><a href="https://bounded-regret.ghost.io/building-technology-to-drive-ai-governance/"><span>great lever for AI governance</span></a><span>.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-vDjBDk2RSiY7M4zoa-1"><p><span>The average human speaks 15,000 words per day; conversatively estimating each message is 10 words, 2.5B messages = 1.7M days = 4500 years. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-vDjBDk2RSiY7M4zoa-1"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-vDjBDk2RSiY7M4zoa-2"><p><span>The canonical term is "identity formation" (</span><a href="https://archive.org/details/identityyouthcri0000erik"><span>Erikson, 1968</span></a><span>); the related concept of the "capacity to be alone" is from </span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/13610513/"><span>Winnicott (1958)</span></a><span>. See </span><a href="https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/psychiatry/articles/10.3389/fpsyt.2023.1179677/full"><span>McVarnock et al. (2023)</span></a><span> for a modern review of how solitude supports identity formation in adolescence. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-vDjBDk2RSiY7M4zoa-2"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KGcE7eAdfxHchk25X/cognitive-security-as-an-ai-safety-cause-area#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Linkpost: New Vatican Encyclical on AI Governance](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ehdv7z7L7ESxtKL4j/linkpost-new-vatican-encyclical-on-ai-governance)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Pope Leo XIV has released a new, 42k-word encyclical laying out the Vatican's position on many AI safety topics.  You can read the full thing </span><a href="https://www.vatican.va/content/leo-xiv/en/encyclicals/documents/20260515-magnifica-humanitas.html"><span>here</span></a><span>, or read the Vatican's press release </span><a href="https://www.vaticannews.va/en/pope/news/2026-05/pope-leo-xiv-encyclical-magnifica-humanitas-ai.html"><span>here</span></a><span>, or </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/05/25/world/europe/pope-leo-encyclical.html"><span>coverage </span></a><span>in the NY Times, or perhaps consider having an LLM read the whole encyclical, then chatting about whatever specifics you're interested in!</span></p><p><span>Below is a portion of the </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/05/25/world/europe/pope-leo-encyclical.html"><span>NY Times story</span></a><span> on the event:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Leo’s declaration outlined his desire to protect human dignity and agency in an age in which technology threatens to replace humans in many professional and social roles. He presented it alongside Christopher Olah, a co-founder of Anthropic, a major A.I. developer, in a symbolic gesture of dialogue between leaders of the spiritual and technological worlds.</span></p><p><span>While emphasizing that “technology should not be considered, in itself, as a force antagonistic to humanity,” he wrote that “the pursuit of greater profits cannot justify choices that systematically sacrifice jobs.”</span></p><p><span>Among other things, Leo called for:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>government regulation of the private companies that are driving the development of A.I.</span></li><li value="2"><span>protection and retraining for workers whose jobs are threatened</span></li><li value="3"><span>education to help students think critically about the technology</span></li><li value="4"><span>action to protect children from violent, hypersexualized or fake information online that is often generated by A.I.</span></li><li value="5"><span>safeguards to ensure that humans, not artificial intelligence, remain responsible for all decisions regarding the use of weapons.</span></li></ul><p><span>Above all he emphasized the importance of retaining a fundamental social role for all human beings. “A society that guarantees employment to only a small fraction of the population, despite having a high level of technical development, risks exposing many to forced inactivity,” he wrote. “This creates a paradox of material progress and anthropological regression that undermines the foundations of a just and stable social peace."</span></p></blockquote><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ehdv7z7L7ESxtKL4j/linkpost-new-vatican-encyclical-on-ai-governance#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Taxing Small Cars To Improve MPG](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7K8Bnn6Pwh5ciXpKR/taxing-small-cars-to-improve-mpg)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>

Cars and trucks are getting bigger, and I had a vague sense that fuel
economy regulations were partly to blame.  Looking into it, it's hard
to say how much is regulations vs people wanting to buy vehicles that
look rugged, but the regulations really aren't helping.

</span>

</p><p>

This chart is the core of it:

</p>

<p>

<a href="https://www.jefftk.com/2026-cafe-mpg-target-curves-big.png"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/7K8Bnn6Pwh5ciXpKR/tetyhwpegs9pjaoyrzby" /></a></p><div></div>

<p></p>

<p>

This is what manufacturers were looking at when they decided to build
today's cars.  To figure out the target fuel economy for a vehicle you
first calculate its "footprint", which is the area between the
wheels.  On <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/shared-car-one-year-in">our</a> 2013 <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honda_Fit">Honda Fit</a> that's
4.8ft side-to-side and 8.2ft front-to-back, for a footprint of 39sqft.
Then you ask if it's a car or truck.  This tells you which curve to
use, and where along it to look.

</p>

<p>

Looking at the chart we can now see why it's hard for Honda to sell a
Fit today.  The best Honda could do for a five-seater non-hybrid
hatchback is maybe a CAFE rating of 44mpg. [1] This puts them 23mpg
short, and if Honda was a one-model car company they'd expect to owe
$3,910/vehicle in fines: $17 per 0.1mpg shortfall.  Since the
regulation is about an average across all the cars they sell the
actual effect is both lower and more complex, and maybe something like
$2k.

</p>

<p>

Aside: the fine structure here is a sad artifact of us thinking in
miles-per-gallon instead of gallons-per-mile.  Going from 25mpg (0.04
gpm) to 50mpg (0.02 gpm) saves as much gas as going from 50mpg (0.02
gpm) to infinite (0 gpm).  But the penalty for being below a target is
calculated on the gap in miles-per-gallon and not gallons-per-mile.
If you miss a 50mpg (0.02gpm) target by hitting 25mpg (0.04gpm), or
miss a 75mpg (0.013gpm) target by hitting 50mpg (0.02gpm), you pay the
same fine even though the first involves burning much more
counterfactual gas: over 10,000 miles the first burns 200 gallons
more than its target while the second only burns 67 more.

</p>

<p>

What did Honda do?  They discontinued the Fit, and replaced it with
the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honda_HR-V#Third_generation_(2021)">HR-V</a>.
It's bigger and heavier, and looks like it was trying to be a "light
truck". Combined with its larger footprint that would give a much
lower target: 49mpg instead of 67mpg.  It still doesn't hit that, but
it's less of a penalty.  And then it doesn't actually count as a
light truck, though I don't know if that was the plan from the beginning or
a compromise they had to accept.

</p>

<p>

Overall, this regulatory structure taxes manufacturers more for
making small low vehicles, the kind that are easiest to make fuel
efficient.  Here's where I would write that this is counterproductive
and we should stop, except we sort of already did.  In 2025 the
penalty for non-compliance was set to $0 as part of the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Big_Beautiful_Bill_Act#Miscellaneous">OBBBA</a>. This
means in some sense manufacturers are free to make small cars and
trucks with achievable mileage.  Except the rest of the structure is
still there, complete with the distorted incentives, and ready to be
reinstated by a future government.

</p>

<p>

If at some point there's political will to improve this situation, and
a carbon tax remains off the table, I'd like to see a return to the
simpler Ford-era system where targets didn't scale with vehicle size.
But then I'd need to understand why they switched to this system (if
it's crash safety we should legislate that directly) and it's not
clear that continued regulatory whiplash is worth it.

</p>

<p>
<br />

[1] The closest to 67mpg would be something like the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honda_Insight#First_generation_(ZE1;_1999)">first-gen
Honda Insight</a>.  This got very close, but seating only two people
with a lightweight construction that would do very poorly in modern
crash testing.  If you're willing to make it a hybrid, which does add
significant cost, it is possible: the the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honda_Fit#Powertrain">Jazz
e:HEV</a> (essentially a hybrid Fourth-generation Fit) would probably
come in around 72mpg.

  </p>

<p><i>Comment via: <a href="https://www.facebook.com/jefftk/posts/pfbid0JKJdhS5Mpn8L6urfbtqx2mAW6iZU7rQrKrBBDHLRiPu4ys3fDxrSrwhg5ysw26Sjl">facebook</a>, <a href="https://mastodon.mit.edu/@jefftk/116631732521533417">mastodon</a>, <a href="https://bsky.app/profile/jefftk.com/post/3mmmwncigvk2d">bluesky</a></i></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7K8Bnn6Pwh5ciXpKR/taxing-small-cars-to-improve-mpg#comments">Discuss</a>

### [A (Slightly) Mechanistic Theory for Exponentially Increasing AI Time Horizons?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zT76JcomKkdqo8tC6/a-slightly-mechanistic-theory-for-exponentially-increasing)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>AI ‘time horizons’ are mostly not about time (I think it’s mostly ‘data’, but you’ll see where I’m unsure).</span></i></p><p><span>One chart from 2025 has become perhaps the most (in)famous in modern AI commentary.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/4ff4fbe4dd92fbd436ba9733f7686982b3dfe50cca9b3f705b4380e441d622d2/gwzrnngbqe7pzr5bpfgb" /><p><span>For those in the know, ‘</span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2025-03-19-measuring-ai-ability-to-complete-long-tasks/"><span>the METR graph</span></a><span>’</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqsix5idegma"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqsix5idegma">[1]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;is unusually compelling because it achieves what so few measures of AI progress have achieved: a </span><i><span>somewhat</span></i><span>&nbsp;meaningful Y axis (‘time horizon’</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefk4ini28n16s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnk4ini28n16s">[2]</a></sup></span><span>) </span><i><span>as well as</span></i><span>&nbsp;a somewhat predictable trend over time! (This is</span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/P8jsAySQzfgkeoDgb/ai-benchmarking-has-a-y-axis-problem"><span>&nbsp;</span></a><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/P8jsAySQzfgkeoDgb/ai-benchmarking-has-a-y-axis-problem"><span>remarkably rare</span></a><span>!)</span></p><p><span>Frustratingly, the only </span><i><span>superficially</span></i><span>&nbsp;available takeaway is something like, ‘the line goes up straight-ish over time’. This is better than nothing, but it’s very dissatisfactory from the point of view of getting confidence in the predictions, because it exposes no deeper mechanism. This drives a lot of confusion and argument about the implications.</span></p><p><span>A deeper mechanism would be good for two reasons:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>It enables a sanity check on the trend, perhaps enabling more confidence in its predictions than we would sensibly allow with only the surface understanding.</span></li><li value="2"><span>It gives some way to interrogate when and how the trend might </span><i><span>change</span></i><span>&nbsp;(because if the deeper mechanism gets deflected, the superficial projection would be broken, but a prediction based on the deeper mechanism might stay viable for longer).</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>(A sub-reason: if we </span><i><span>want</span></i><span>&nbsp;the trend to change, knowing some more mechanism might shed light on some levers to pull rather than sitting around to wait and see.)</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>As an analogy, a similarly superficial trend,</span><a href="https://ourworldindata.org/moores-law"><span>&nbsp;</span></a><a href="https://ourworldindata.org/moores-law"><span>Moore’s Law</span></a><span>, can be a little better mechanistically explained by the more general</span><a href="https://ourworldindata.org/learning-curve"><span>&nbsp;</span></a><a href="https://ourworldindata.org/learning-curve"><span>Wright’s Law</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdqjok035v4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndqjok035v4">[3]</a></sup></span><span>. This is great, because that law covers more cases, and it can handle some deflection from the trend, or give some idea of when (and under what conditions) the trend might break. Important when looking at plausible futures, and how to steer toward desirable ones!</span></p><h2><span>Attempting to find some mechanism in the METR graph</span></h2><h3><span>Task ‘length’ and success modelling</span></h3><p><span>Why did METR focus on ‘task length’?</span></p><p><span>First, it’s not how long the AI agent takes. It’s how long the task in question takes a panel of sampled human experts, on average</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefm0n4ptok6hg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnm0n4ptok6hg">[4]</a></sup></span><span>. So in their ‘time horizon’ measurements, METR is capturing the </span><i><span>effective hours of human-expert-equivalent activity</span></i><span>&nbsp;that AI agents can carry out.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefawlcsw9rij"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnawlcsw9rij">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>One way to think about the time it takes human experts to complete a task is that, for each </span><i><span>subtask</span></i><span>&nbsp;they had to know how to do (or be able to figure out how to do) and then successfully execute, the overall task takes incrementally longer. By how much? That depends on exactly what ‘subtasks’ we're imagining breaking things down into.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnkg33ahcelp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnkg33ahcelp">[6]</a></sup></span><span> But </span><i><span>on average</span></i><span>&nbsp;longer tasks correspond to more distinct challenges, all else equal.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefq5s3w1s8mpq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnq5s3w1s8mpq">[7]</a></sup></span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zT76JcomKkdqo8tC6/d27d6bee0f252d1bac45930796386c78c788ec2b57620715f4d2bd32e90ac4a1/milmsztmtrl8vvygvrfl" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>A random generation of tasks (rows) with ‘subtasks’ as segments, sorted by subtask count from least to most. You can see that the more subtasks, the longer, on average. It’s a little ragged — not all subtasks are the same length, so occasionally fewer, longer subtasks add up to more overall time than more, shorter subtasks. What METR can easily measure is the overall duration. Even if the subtask division is somewhat subjectively defined, duration stands as a reasonable proxy for it. Note that the vertical subtask count axis is sorted but not uniformly spaced. (Created with claude.ai.)</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span>This is the first piece of mechanism we should take into account.</span></b><span>&nbsp;‘Time’ is not </span><i><span>agent time</span></i><span>: it's a noisy estimate for ‘number of somewhat challenging requirements necessary to complete the task’.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7h9q14ug7yc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7h9q14ug7yc">[8]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>This is treating </span><i><span>overall</span></i><span>&nbsp;tasks as formed by something like drawing ‘subtasks’ out of a large collection of possible requirements. Given the agent’s general competence, specific knowledge, tools available, and opportunity to retry or learn on the fly, sometimes the agent can meet these requirements. Other times it can’t.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefh92sfsa6m2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnh92sfsa6m2">[9]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;‘Longer’ tasks simply draw more subtasks (that’s why they’re ‘longer’, in this model: expert humans had more subtasks they needed to carry out).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefesfoi8c5y1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnesfoi8c5y1">[10]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Toby Ord </span><a href="https://www.tobyord.com/writing/half-life"><span>demonstrates one way to take this intuition further</span></a><span>, noting that if we explicitly model overall success </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> according to a simple model where chance of failure compounds with task ‘length’, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, we get a reasonable fit for the data METR collected. (Interestingly Toby mainly seems to continue treating this as ‘agent time’. I’ll instead take as given&nbsp;that we’re talking about a proxy for number&nbsp;of subtasks.)</span></p><p><span>In other words, for a given AI agent and task domain, there's something like a ‘hazard rate’, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (per-subtask probability of failure), which reasonably well summarises (and predicts) the AI's level of success in that domain:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>(i.e. to succeed at a </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-step task, the agent must </span><i><span>not fail</span></i><span>&nbsp;— must </span><i><span>avoid</span></i><span>&nbsp;the ‘hazard’ </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> — </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> times.)</span></p><p><span>This enables us to translate back and forth between an estimate of this hazard rate </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and an estimate of a ‘half-life’ or 50% success horizon — how ‘long’ (i.e. complex) a task needs to be before the agent fails more often than not — and also to extrapolate to ‘durations’ corresponding to other reliability levels, like 99% or 99.9%</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpfaffol1y7i"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpfaffol1y7i">[11]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>In this formulation, the hazard rate, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, stands in for what fraction of our ‘subtask’ pool the agent can’t (yet) succeed at, which ends up being a reasonable summary of the agent’s competence in this domain.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref30pjgkpgu3g"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn30pjgkpgu3g">[12]</a></sup></span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zT76JcomKkdqo8tC6/c0658e82b6ae021a2145e9d655c7c1c5160f73893841cc4272cacfb5e16fc17f/uuof2fzbczxakpxvd3ks" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>This time, we’re looking at overall task success as if the agent has a 98% chance of meeting any particular subtask’s requirements. Sometimes a shorter task will happen to have one of the difficult subtasks — but usually they’re overall successful. As tasks get longer, there’s a greater chance that at least one subtask requirement is insurmountable at this reliability level. Among longer tasks, overall success becomes fewer and farther between. This agent can’t expect to often succeed on tasks longer than 50 or so subtasks.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>If you have a new task, you don’t know if the agent has all it needs to complete it. But the task ‘length’ is an indicator of how many tricky subtasks it has, and similar-lengthed tasks will have similar numbers of such subtasks — so their average success rate is a good estimate for how likely the agent is to succeed at this new task.</span></p><h3><span>Relating hazard rate with frontier AI development</span></h3><p><span>METR's graph is compelling because it suggests a steadily increasing frontier of success horizon as AI developers produce new agents over time.</span></p><p><span>What does this imply if we interrogate our hazard rate model? Well, 'half-life' (and indeed various success-level horizons) is observed apparently growing exponentially with date </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>This is the central striking takeaway from the METR graph (modulo their measurement uncertainty). Half-life go up!</span></p><p><span>But half-life according to our model has:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the per-step hazard rate from before. When this </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is not too close to 1, that half-life is, fairly intuitively, approximately proportional to the reciprocal of the hazard rate:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>So </span><b><span>METR's observation of rising time horizons is equivalent to saying that the frontier </span></b><i><b><span>hazard rate</span></b></i><b><span>&nbsp;is </span></b><i><b><span>shrinking</span></b></i><b><span>&nbsp;exponentially over time</span></b><span>.</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>Recall that this hazard rate corresponds with the fraction of ‘subtasks’ in a domain that an agent doesn’t yet know how to complete. So this fraction is presumed to shrink roughly exponentially with date, in turn driving the observed ‘longer’ success horizons.</span></p><h3><span>Why does hazard rate shrink with date?</span></h3><p><span>Here’s where to look for the next bit of mechanism. Why would the hazard rate, the fraction of ‘subtasks’ which remain out of reach, shrink in that way?</span></p><p><span>It goes without saying that AI developers are chasing after increasing competence in their products, so (if they are doing anything at all right!) the direction of movement is unsurprising. Why that particular roughly-exponential form, though?</span></p><p><span>I confess here I’m uncertain and the quest for more mechanism&nbsp;continues.</span></p><p><b><span>My best guess is that it’s about the </span></b><i><b><span>effective evidence</span></b></i><b><span>&nbsp;available to the agent toward subtask solution strategy.</span></b><span>&nbsp;Intuitively, if you’ve seen </span><i><span>very similar</span></i><span>&nbsp;subtasks </span><i><span>many</span></i><span>&nbsp;times before, it’s hard to go too wrong. If you’ve only seen </span><i><span>vaguely</span></i><span>&nbsp;similar subtasks once or twice, you’re in much less familiar territory and stand a good chance of stalling. Suggestively, effective evidence and training data are both </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_content"><i><span>information-like</span></i></a><span>&nbsp;quantities, but I don’t want to make too much of that without a crisper connection. Formally, we could consider how many </span><i><span>bits</span></i><span>&nbsp;of evidence the agent can muster about how to proceed (either from past learning or by </span><a href="https://www.oliversourbut.net/p/you-cant-skip-exploration"><span>exploring in context</span></a><span>).</span></p><p><span>In other words, </span><i><span>training</span></i><span>&nbsp;produces </span><i><span>learnings</span></i><span>. These range from broad, </span><i><span>generally-applicable</span></i><span>&nbsp;heuristics for adaptable, effective behaviour (experiment, test your work, notice when something surprising happens, read the manual if you can find one, accrue power and resources at any opportunity, ...), to narrow </span><i><span>specific</span></i><span>&nbsp;details about particular situations and activities (Earth's radius is roughly 6.4 megameters, detonating TNT yields roughly 4.2 kJ/g, humans succumb to oxygen deprivation after around 5 minutes, …). Ahem.</span></p><p><span>Empirically, AI developers have historically poured </span><i><span>something like</span></i><span>&nbsp;exponentially increasing ‘quantities’ of ‘data’ into their machine learning pipelines.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefahrpdoswwk8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnahrpdoswwk8">[13]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;Mathematically, that implies a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_law"><i><span>power law</span></i></a><span>: data inputs </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> rising at one exponential rate, matched by hazard rate </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> decaying at another exponential rate.</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>Power laws aren’t </span><i><span>deeply</span></i><span>&nbsp;mechanically explanatory, but they’re often the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neural_scaling_law"><span>best we have</span></a><span>&nbsp;in machine learning, and are at least more predictable than mere date-based trends. Under the simple subtask model described here, this power law translates directly into a power law between ‘time horizon’ and data. This is actually the same level of explanatory improvement offered by Wright’s Law over Moore’s: not fully mechanistic, but an extra layer of detail which offers firmer purchase on what’s going on.</span></p><p><span>What this doesn’t straightforwardly account for is the benefit to success rates of increased </span><i><span>in-context reasoning</span></i><span>, which is exhibited according to METR’s estimates. I expect this is operating on those borderline subtasks — where the agent would have some slim chance of satisfying them if it ‘rushed’. In those cases, ‘</span><a href="https://www.oliversourbut.net/p/better-than-logarithmic-returns-to"><span>thinking harder</span></a><span>’ may more effectively recall and combine the relevant learned knowledge, and allow better choices for </span><a href="https://www.oliversourbut.net/p/you-cant-skip-exploration"><span>exploratory discovery in situ</span></a><span>. In any case, changing the thinking budget of an otherwise similar existing system certainly calls for a more mechanistic understanding than mere date-based trend extrapolation!</span></p><p><span>I would be thrilled if someone with more smarts, time to experiment, and access to data were to dig into ways we could match up various AI production inputs (especially ‘data’ in various forms) with observed outputs like ‘time horizon’. One of the more difficult pieces might be quantifying ‘data’, especially teasing apart what types of evidence are ‘relevant’ for the domain and tasks at hand.</span></p><h2><span>Upshot</span></h2><p><span>The kind-of-boring upshot of this is that data and ‘practice’ on related tasks makes AI better at those tasks! This is boring because, </span><i><span>well obviously!,</span></i><span>&nbsp;we already basically knew that. But it’s encouraging because we can say a little more than that, which gives us some better grasp on what’s driving ‘time horizon’ progress in particular domains — and it can help get more precise about predictions.</span></p><p><span>The fact that the ‘subtask’ model — with a ‘hazard rate’ of subtasks currently out of reach — is a fairly explanatory fit for capability profiles of individual agents is evidence that there’re not </span><i><span>unusual</span></i><span>&nbsp;amounts of generalisation capability in AI. As with humans, they can extrapolate a bit, but need ‘experience’ and examples to succeed.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefn5hyqu5z7zl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnn5hyqu5z7zl">[14]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;Importantly, this means that </span><b><span>vast </span></b><i><b><span>in silico</span></b></i><b><span>&nbsp;training ranges for software, cyber, and mathematics very likely </span></b><i><b><span>won’t</span></b></i><b><span>&nbsp;transfer much to other domains of interest</span></b><span>, like interpersonal intelligence, medical discovery, bioweapons development, intelligence analysis, and robotic manipulation. Of course, like with every domain of human experience and activity, we have </span><i><span>some</span></i><span>&nbsp;relevantly-similar data already collected, and schemes can be devised to more rapidly </span><i><span>expand</span></i><span>&nbsp;that digitised experience bank for AI to learn from. Increasing adoption of AI in task-integrated contexts, industrial deployment, and even explicit approaches to gathering example data such as ‘</span><a href="https://www.technologyreview.com/2026/04/01/1134863/humanoid-data-training-gig-economy-2026-breakthrough-technology/"><span>hand movement farming</span></a><span>’ are the leading indicators to watch for progress in particular domains — not just the headline benchmark metrics in software-like tasks.</span></p><p><span>For some types of activity, developers are probably ‘running out’ of raw example data to scrape from the internet. The </span><a href="https://www.oliversourbut.net/p/how-did-large-language-models-get"><span>era of mostly-pretraining</span></a><span>&nbsp;is over. For domains which can be relatively easily verified, like mathematics and coding, this is </span><a href="https://helentoner.substack.com/i/161853197/what-makes-reasoning-models-so-good-at-math-and-code"><span>very surmountable</span></a><span>&nbsp;— you can just run drills galore on a computer and get data that way. But this costs extra compute and </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/gradient-updates/how-far-can-reasoning-models-scale"><span>doesn’t scale at the </span></a><a href="https://epoch.ai/gradient-updates/how-far-can-reasoning-models-scale"><i><span>same</span></i></a><a href="https://epoch.ai/gradient-updates/how-far-can-reasoning-models-scale"><span>&nbsp;exponential rate for long</span></a><span>&nbsp;(perhaps 10x/year presently). As soon as this year, developers could be back to ‘only’ </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/blog/training-compute-of-frontier-ai-models-grows-by-4-5x-per-year"><span>scaling compute around 4x per year</span></a><span>&nbsp;(and a bit after that they might have </span><a href="https://epochai.substack.com/p/frontier-labs-dont-use-most-ai-compute"><span>bought most of the compute</span></a><span>! — and will only be able to scale at the positively sloth-like </span><a href="https://ourworldindata.org/moores-law"><span>1.5x-ish a year of underlying hardware progress</span></a><span>). I don’t feel confident extrapolating exactly where that cashes out, but if the data-driven subtask-learning model is right, it would imply </span><b><span>we should see less steepness to the time horizon growth quite soon</span></b><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkw48aceft7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkw48aceft7">[15]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Some commentaries project that, once AI can autonomously do software and machine learning work reliably, it will thereafter enter a ‘recursive self-improvement’ phase and rapidly colonise </span><i><span>all</span></i><span>&nbsp;capabilities. I don’t think this is missing the point entirely: there will be modest multipliers on the speed of the AI development pipeline, and we might see an ‘explosion’ in the </span><i><span>speed</span></i><span>&nbsp;and </span><i><span>cost-effectiveness</span></i><span>&nbsp;of AI (because they are among the most immediately-verifiable properties to iterate on). But </span><b><span>generalisation doesn’t come for free, so on-task data and compute will remain crucial to </span></b><i><b><span>broadening</span></b></i><b><span>&nbsp;the frontier of autonomous capabilities.</span></b><span>&nbsp;</span><i><span>Collecting</span></i><span>&nbsp;that data and </span><i><span>manufacturing</span></i><span>&nbsp;that compute&nbsp;look to me like the rate-limiting steps, and therefore the major leading indicators to use in foresight. The best case I can make for a much more general explosion is if the speed and cost-effectiveness explosions rapidly accelerate the gathering and digestion of diverse task data — but I think that remains mostly rate-limited in the familiar ways: some domains easy and some more difficult. Don’t mistake me for ruling out across-the-board AI capability! Companies are charging ahead with data collection and set on automating much of their AI production pipeline. It just won’t happen overnight.</span></p><p><i><span>Thanks to Coz Ududec for a conversation prompting me to think about this.</span></i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqsix5idegma"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqsix5idegma">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Produced by AI monitoring non-profit </span><a href="https://metr.org/"><span>METR</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnk4ini28n16s"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefk4ini28n16s">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Very importantly, it’s measured </span><i><span>within a particular collection of challenges/tasks</span></i><span>&nbsp;which are mostly associated with software development, especially ML engineering. METR also has</span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2025-07-14-how-does-time-horizon-vary-across-domains/"><span>&nbsp;</span></a><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2025-07-14-how-does-time-horizon-vary-across-domains/"><span>a great preliminary study</span></a><span>&nbsp;of some </span><i><span>other</span></i><span>&nbsp;domains, finding differing, but perhaps also somewhat predictable trends.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndqjok035v4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdqjok035v4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://ourworldindata.org/moores-law"><span>Moore’s Law</span></a><span>&nbsp;is the very superficial observation that, over time, the number of transistors per chip doubles roughly every two years. (More recently, it’s been more clearly expressed as the price per transistor</span><a href="https://ourworldindata.org/grapher/costs-of-66-different-technologies-over-time?country=~Transistor"><span>&nbsp;</span></a><a href="https://ourworldindata.org/grapher/costs-of-66-different-technologies-over-time?country=~Transistor"><span>halving every year-or-two</span></a><span>.)</span></p><p><a href="https://ourworldindata.org/learning-curve"><span>Wright’s Law</span></a><span>&nbsp;is the slightly more mechanistic and general observation that production of many commodities follows ‘learning curves’, such that each doubling of cumulative production produces roughly similar relative cost savings. (We can in turn attempt to explain this in yet more mechanistic terms, pointing to the insight gained from observing and recording many trials and experiments, with suitably diminishing returns.)</span></p><p><span>Now, </span><i><span>if</span></i><span>&nbsp;the quantity demanded and produced grows exponentially over time (as it has for computer chips), </span><i><span>then</span></i><span>&nbsp;Wright’s Law predicts comparable cost savings each year: Moore’s Law. If the quantity produced grows (or shrinks) in some </span><i><span>other</span></i><span>&nbsp;pattern over time, Wright’s Law, by accounting for this mechanistic detail, can</span><a href="https://web.mit.edu/mitssrc/nsf/papers/Nagy_Farmer_Bui_Trancik_2013.pdf"><span>&nbsp;</span></a><a href="https://web.mit.edu/mitssrc/nsf/papers/Nagy_Farmer_Bui_Trancik_2013.pdf"><span>often forecast cost trends more reliably</span></a><span>&nbsp;than Moore’s.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnm0n4ptok6hg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefm0n4ptok6hg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Also note that the estimation of ‘task length’ according to human experts was quite crude (naturally, humans are the most expensive part of most experiments!), and there are good reasons to</span><a href="https://www.transformernews.ai/p/against-the-metr-graph-coding-capabilities-software-jobs-task-ai"><span>&nbsp;</span></a><a href="https://www.transformernews.ai/p/against-the-metr-graph-coding-capabilities-software-jobs-task-ai"><span>treat the reported error bars as much too narrow</span></a><span>, i.e. misleadingly confident. I’ll use quotes around ‘time’ related quantities in this post as a reminder that it’s a loose estimate of a crudely human-performer-derived time-to-completion for tasks, and doesn’t correspond well to real time as such.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnawlcsw9rij"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefawlcsw9rij">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I don’t know if METR publishes how long the agents themselves take at these tasks — I don’t think so, and it’d arguably be ill-defined anyway since it would depend in part on how fast a computer you ran the agent on.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnkg33ahcelp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnkg33ahcelp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>If we conceptually carve up subtasks into smaller pieces, they'll be quicker per piece, but there are commensurably more of them, and vice versa.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnq5s3w1s8mpq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefq5s3w1s8mpq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This could come apart if longer tasks are systematically more likely to include repetitive </span><i><span>similar</span></i><span>&nbsp;activities rather than a series of distinct ones, for example. Or longer tasks might tend to admit more truly alternative pathways. Both these effects could make longer tasks slightly easier than the naive picture. There are also higher-level ‘orchestration’ tasks i.e. coherently coming up with (and executing and adapting) an appropriate sequential plan: perhaps these might be systematically more </span><i><span>difficult</span></i><span>&nbsp;for longer tasks.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7h9q14ug7yc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7h9q14ug7yc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Notably, agents sometimes take a (relatively) longer time to do something that’s quicker for humans, and vice versa.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnh92sfsa6m2"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefh92sfsa6m2">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Incidentally, success (or not) here already accounts for the agent attempting and re-attempting steps or fixing earlier mistakes, which might take variable amounts of time: another reason not to treat this as </span><i><span>agent</span></i><span>&nbsp;time. Some subtasks might be intermediate and succeed </span><i><span>sometimes</span></i><span>&nbsp;(for example if the agent can’t easily choose the best approach but sometimes hits on the right one, or sometimes gets stuck in a terminal cycle but sometimes makes lucky progress.)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnesfoi8c5y1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefesfoi8c5y1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is throwing away some detail: obviously not all subtasks are equally likely to follow from each other! There’s some correspondence between on-task sequences. But within a particular domain (like software engineering), this naive model of overall tasks combining subtasks somewhat randomly seems to do OK.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpfaffol1y7i"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpfaffol1y7i">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>By the way, the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rule_of_72"><span>rule of 72</span></a><span>&nbsp;provides a really quick mental approximation for the higher-reliability ‘time’ horizons, depending on the ‘half-life’ (the 50% ‘time’ horizon).</span></p><p><span>Divide the ‘half-life’ by 72. That’s the 1% </span><i><span>failure</span></i><span>&nbsp;horizon (equivalent to the 99% </span><i><span>success</span></i><span>&nbsp;horizon). Multiply by your target </span><i><span>failure</span></i><span>&nbsp;rate in percent, and you’re done: that’s your target success ‘time’ horizon. E.g. if ‘half-life’ is 1h, the ‘time’ horizon at 99.9% is (1h/72)*(0.1) i.e. 5 seconds.</span></p><p><span>(This also reveals that cutting the ‘time’ horizon tenfold cuts the average failure rate tenfold and so on.)</span></p><p><span>Going the other way, estimating long-horizon success rates, divide your target horizon by the ‘half-life’. That’s how many halvings of success to expect: raise one half to that power for your success rate. E.g. if ‘half-life’ is 1h, your 24h success rate is </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> i.e. one in sixteen million.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn30pjgkpgu3g"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref30pjgkpgu3g">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It didn’t have to be that way! A single number which manages to explain a lot of variation in agent capability is very suggestive of an underlying mechanism something like the ‘fraction of subtasks’ model I’ve described here. Of course there is still some residual uncertainty and there may be better summaries available with a more detailed model or epicycles on this one.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnahrpdoswwk8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefahrpdoswwk8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This may recently be trickier to measure as training pipelines have adapted to </span><a href="https://www.oliversourbut.net/p/reinforcement-learning-scaling-might"><span>incorporate more reinforcement learning</span></a><span>, which means these experience data are less ‘homogeneously slurped up from the internet’ and increasingly ‘proactively curated from in-domain training curricula’. So the mere quantity of data isn’t like-for-like over time.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnn5hyqu5z7zl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefn5hyqu5z7zl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In fact contemporary AI is perhaps substantially </span><i><span>less</span></i><span>&nbsp;good at generalisation than humans, though I’d like to be better informed about how factors like sample efficiency of AI learning (including in-context learning) stack up.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkw48aceft7"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkw48aceft7">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Actually saying something so bearish about AI makes me nervous, as there is a venerable history of people boldly declaring AI is </span><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2021214568891154722"><span>about to hit a wall</span></a><span>! But I think it’s borne out. I’m not saying progress </span><i><span>stops</span></i><span>, I’m saying it probably gets slower (in exponential terms).</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zT76JcomKkdqo8tC6/a-slightly-mechanistic-theory-for-exponentially-increasing#comments">Discuss</a>

---

## C++

### [CppCon 2025 Could C++ Developers Handle an ABI Break Today? -- Luis Caro Campos](https://isocpp.org//blog/2026/05/cppcon-2025-could-cpp-developers-handle-an-abi-break-today-luis-caro-campos)

**来源**: ISO C++ Blog

**摘要**: <p>
	<img alt="campos-break.png" src="https://isocpp.org/files/img/campos-break.png" style="width: 400px; margin: 10px; float: right;" />Registration is now open for CppCon 2026!&nbsp;The conference starts on September 12 and will be held&nbsp;<a href="https://cppcon.org/">in person in Aurora, CO</a>. To whet your appetite for this year&rsquo;s conference, we&rsquo;re posting videos of some of the top-rated talks from last year's conference. Here&rsquo;s another CppCon talk video we hope you will enjoy &ndash; and why not&nbsp;<a href="https://cppcon.org/registration/"><strong>register today</strong></a><strong>&nbsp;for CppCon 2026!</strong></p>
<blockquote>
	<h3>
		<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VbSKnvldtbs">Could C++ Developers Handle an ABI Break Today?</a></h3>
	<p>
		by Luis Caro Campos</p>
</blockquote>
<p>
	Summary of the talk:</p>
<blockquote>
	<p>
		The C++ Evolution Working Group recently reaffirmed its commitment to ABI stability, prioritizing link compatibility with C and older C++. The C++11 libstdc++ ABI updates introduced in gcc 5.1, although not strictly &ldquo;breaking&rdquo;, are still in the collective memory of C++ developers and this experience shows us how sensitive the ecosystem is to ABI updates.<br />
		<br />
		This talk challenges the assumption that a future ABI break would be equally problematic, as the landscape has evolved significantly in the last decade.<br />
		<br />
		On the one hand, the C++ standard has evolved in such a way that even if the standards committee and compiler vendors go through great lengths to avoid breaking the ABI of standard library implementations, library authors are not as cautious - so in practice, the ability to link objects built with different C++ standard levels does not hold true for a lot of cases.<br />
		<br />
		On the other hand, tooling has evolved significantly in this time period. For example, both Conan and vcpkg are able to &ldquo;tag&rdquo; binaries on some arbitrary ABI version. We can see similar examples in other tools or ecosystems that need to work around ABI complexities.<br />
		<br />
		This talk is not intended to argue about the merits or risks of future ABI changes - but to ask ourselves the question: are we overestimating the pain of a future ABI break?</p>
</blockquote>

## Engineering

### [Bliki: Vibe Coding](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/VibeCoding.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Vibe coding is building a software application by prompting an LLM, telling it
  what to build, trying it out, prompting for changes - but without looking at
  any of the code that the LLM generates. This technique can be used by people
  without any knowledge of programming. However the resulting software often
  shows problems with maintainability, correctness, and security - so is best
  used for disposable software written for a limited audience.</p>

<p>The term was coined in February 2025 by Andrej Karpathy, an experienced
  programmer, in a post on X:</p>

<blockquote>
<p>There's a new kind of coding I call “vibe coding”, where you fully give
    in to the vibes, embrace exponentials, and forget that the code even exists.
    It's possible because the LLMs (e.g. Cursor Composer w Sonnet) are getting
    too good. Also I just talk to Composer with SuperWhisper so I barely even
    touch the keyboard. I ask for the dumbest things like “decrease the padding
    on the sidebar by half” because I'm too lazy to find it. I “Accept All”
    always, I don't read the diffs anymore. When I get error messages I just
    copy paste them in with no comment, usually that fixes it. The code grows
    beyond my usual comprehension, I'd have to really read through it for a
    while. Sometimes the LLMs can't fix a bug so I just work around it or ask
    for random changes until it goes away. It's not too bad for throwaway
    weekend projects, but still quite amusing. I'm building a project or webapp,
    but it's not really coding - I just see stuff, say stuff, run stuff, and
    copy paste stuff, and it mostly works.</p>

<p class="quote-attribution">-- <a href="https://x.com/karpathy/status/1886192184808149383">Andrej Karpathy</a></p>
</blockquote>

<p>The key point about vibe coding is <b>“forget that the code even exists”</b>.
  This is what gives it much of its usefulness, but also its limitations.</p>

<p>Since the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/NovemberInflection.html">November Inflection</a> many programmers are
  getting LLMs to write all their code, commenting that they may never write a
  line of code directly again. However they do care about this code, reviewing
  it, paying attention to its internal structure. In that case, they aren't
  forgetting the code exists, so it's really a different thing that I call
  <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticProgramming.html">Agentic Programming</a>. Sadly the term “vibe coding” really
  caught on, so many people use it to mean agentic programming. However I feel
  that despite this rapid <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/SemanticDiffusion.html">Semantic Diffusion</a>, it's worth
  trying to keep the concepts of vibe coding and agentic programming separate,
  as they are both different to use and different in their consequences.</p>

<p>Because a vibe coder doesn't look at the code, they don't need programming
  skills, so it's perfect for someone with no programming knowledge to build
  applications for their own use. Experienced programmers may also find it handy
  for rapid development of disposable software or prototypes.</p>

<p>Vibe coding
  is still new, so we are exploring its limitations, and those limitations
  change as the sophistication of models and their <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/harness-engineering.html">harnesses</a> change. These
  limitations do introduce considerable risks, particularly if the vibed
  software is used widely or has access to sensitive information.</p>

<p>Perhaps the most serious risk is that of security. LLMs are inherently
  vulnerable as they provide a large attack surface for predators. Vibe coded
  applications can often expose sensitive information or worse, credentials to
  attack deeper into an organization's systems. Even non-programmers need to be
  aware of the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/agentic-ai-security.html#lethal-trifecta">Lethal
  Trifecta</a>.</p>

<p>With little attention to the code, vibed software can rapidly produce
  many lines of code of a very low quality. Such code makes it difficult, even for an LLM,
  to modify and enhance the software in the future. While it's possible that
  growing LLM capabilities will allow it to work with even the largest bowls of
  spaghetti software, thus far it seems clear that well-structured software
  makes life easier for LLMs too.</p>

<p>LLMs are famous for habit of hallucinating incorrect facts and presenting
  these with great confidence. This habit also leads them to create software
  that behaves incorrectly - and those errors may not be manifest to the user.
  Furthermore the non-determinism of LLMs means that it's likely that asking an
  LLM to enhance some software could easily lead it to introduce errors, even in
  parts of the code that shouldn't change due to the new request. We should thus
  treat LLM-generated software with skepticism, it can still be useful, but we
  need to be aware of the risks.</p>

<p>On the whole vibe coding software is best used for disposable software
  that's only used by its author or a close group of collaborators who
  understand and accept the risks involved. Code that is more complex, more
  widely-used, and with more consequences to its risks should not be forgotten about.</p>

---

### [Three more static code analysis sensors](https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents.html#StaticCodeAnalysisDependencyRules)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents.html#StaticCodeAnalysisDependencyRules"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p><b class="author">Birgitta B&#xf6;ckeler</b> adds discussion of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents.html#StaticCodeAnalysisDependencyRules">three more
      sensors</a> for static code analysis, focusing on checking and enforcing
      better modularity. Computational sensors for dependency checks were good
      at enforcing rules, but the rules were limited. Building a computational
      sensor for coupling data proved lackluster. Prompting an inferential
      sensor to review modularity was more effective.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents.html#StaticCodeAnalysisDependencyRules">more…</a></p>

---

### [Maintainability sensors for coding agents](https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>In her recent article about harness engineering for coding agent users,
      <b class="author">Birgitta B&#xf6;ckeler</b> laid out a mental model for expanding a
      coding agent harness: a system of guides and sensors that increase the
      probability of good agent outputs and enable self-correction before issues
      reach human eyes. Birgitta has now started publishing an article where she
      walks though <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents.html">her experiences using sensors</a> to keep a codebase
      maintainable. This part looks at static analysis with basic code linting.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents.html">more…</a></p>
